summary: 'UBS profiles 100 private AI and software firms (one-page profiles: sector, competitors, customers, size, growth, M&A, last round) plus key takeaways from its July private-company conference. UBS says the revenue of these 100 privates, excluding frontier labs, sums to about $62B, with 21 above $1B; incumbency matters less and ''in many cases, disruptors are being chosen over software incumbents'', but outside AI coding fewer privates have scaled in applications. Conference notes: token optimization dominates customer conversations; few AI-natives cited customers displacing incumbent SaaS, and few SaaS incumbents reported material success selling AI suites. Agent-relevant profiles: Sierra ($200M ARR June 2026, growth supported partly by outcome-based pricing; Horizon long-horizon agents), Decagon ($100M ARR per Newcomer; Business Insider says it hired a bank to explore a sale), Serval (platform fee plus workflow consumption, not tokens or seats), Clay (>$100M ARR, NRR over 200%),
  Cognition ($1B ARR from $37M in May 2025), Harvey (>$400M ARR, 25,000+ custom agents), Glean ($300M ARR from subscriptions and consumption), EliseAI, Abridge, Hebbia, n8n, Workato, LangChain, Factory, Saviynt and Modal. Company figures are mostly company-reported or press-reported, labeled per profile.'

 karl.keirstead@ubs.com
 Summary +1-310-734 2455
 In this report we refresh the list of 100 privately-held AI & software firms that are coming Nicholas Hodge
 up most in our industry checks, including one-page profiles on each. These 100 privates Associate Analyst
 represent the next generation of AI & software firms for public tech investors to monitor, nicholas.hodge@ubs.com
 highlighting new growth vectors and potential disruption risk for incumbent public +1-212-882 5954
 software firms. Reflecting the rapid pace of change in the sector, a full 40% of these Samantha Meadows, PhD
 profiles are new compared to the last version from November 2025. Analyst
 samantha.meadows@ubs.com
 Key Observations +1-212-713 2482
 We’d note: 1. The scale of many of these AI & software firms, and more importantly their
 growth rates (which in many cases have accelerated YTD), is a strong signal that
 incumbency matters less in periods of rapid change (now) than it does in periods of
 stability (pre-2023). Vendor choices are instead coming down to who has the best AI
 products or harnesses (a reflection of AI engineering talent as well as the advantage of
 building off a “clean slate”), the best pricing model and interoperability with other parts
 of the AI stack. In many cases, disruptors are being chosen over software incumbents.
 The revenue base of the AI-centric privates is likely now much larger than the AI revenue
 streams of the public players. 2. A quick glance at this Private 100 list reveals that a
 growing portion of the larger privates are operating at the AI infrastructure and coding
 layers, which, combined with the model providers, have captured a very large portion of
 the incremental enterprise spending growth. Outside of the AI coding market, fewer of
 these privates have scaled in the applications space, but this should change over time as
 adoption moves well beyond general chatbots and coding agents. 3. We expect even
 more consolidation, as we’ve already seen with Cursor, Moveworks, Weights & Biases,
 Fin, Hugging Face and other deals as publicly-traded incumbents acquire their way to
 larger AI scale.

 Two Relevant UBS Events
 For investors doing more work around the disruptive privates, we’d flag two upcoming
 annual events that may be of interest:
  Nov 30-Dec 3, 2026 – UBS Tech & AI Conference: Hosted annually at the
 Phoenician hotel in Scottsdale, AZ, the largest UBS conference of the year in the
 US brings together 350+ technology firms and 1,000+ tech investors for on-stage
 firesides as well as small group meetings for investors. The private firms presenting
lilly@smithpointcapital.com - Smith Point Capital - 28/10/2026 04:44 PM UTC

 this year include OpenAI, Databricks, Perplexity, ElevenLabs, Glean, Higgsfield and
 1Password among many others.
  Jan 13-14, 2027 – UBS Venture Capital Summit: Each year, UBS brings together
 15+ of the largest venture capital (VC) firms and their senior partners or founders
 focused on enterprise tech for two days of firesides on emerging trends in
 technology and the portfolio companies that these VC firms are investing behind.
 The 2027 edition will again be hosted at the Rosewood Sand Hill hotel in Menlo
 Park, CA.

 AI Research UBS Research

 Key Observations
 We’d note: 1. The scale of many of these AI and software firms, and more importantly
 their growth rates (which in many cases have accelerated YTD), is a strong signal that
 incumbency matters less in periods of rapid change (now) than it does in periods of
 stability (pre-2023). Vendor choices are instead coming down to who has the best AI
 products or harnesses (a reflection of AI engineering talent as well as the advantage of
 building off a “clean slate”), the best pricing model and interoperability with other parts
 of the AI stack. In many cases, disruptors are being chosen over software incumbents.
 The revenue base of the AI-centric privates is likely now much larger than the AI revenue
 streams of the public players. 2. A quick glance at this Private 100 list reveals that a
 growing portion of the larger privates are operating at the AI infrastructure and coding
 layers, which, combined with the model providers, have captured a very large portion of
 the incremental enterprise spending growth. Outside of the AI coding market, fewer of
 these privates have scaled in the applications space, but this should change over time as
 adoption moves well beyond general chatbots and coding agents. 3. We expect even
 more consolidation, as we’ve already seen with Cursor, Moveworks, Weights & Biases,
 Fin, Hugging Face and other deals as publicly-traded incumbents acquire their way to
 larger AI scale.

 The Scale of Privates
 The first key observation is the sheer scale of the privately-held AI & Software firms. As
 we illustrate in Figure 1 below, the revenues of just these 100 privates – excluding the
 frontier labs - sum to ~$62 billion, with 21 of these 100 firms having estimated revenues
 that exceed $1 billion. Consider this – ~$62 billion of revenue is the equivalent of ~17
 Snowflake’s or Datadog’s in the private markets. This is unprecedented, clearly a
 reflection of an extraordinary demand backdrop for especially AI-centric technology and
 services combined with ample funding available for privates over the last 5+ years that
 has reduced the motivation to go public.

 AI Research 22 October 2026 ab 7
 UBS Private AI Conference Takes
 In mid-July, UBS hosted our 5th annual Private AI & Software Conference in Menlo Park,
 CA, where we hosted 80+ top private companies (AlphaSense, Anduril, ClickHouse,
 Cohesity, Cognition, Crusoe, Cursor, ElevenLabs, Factory AI, Fireworks, Glean, Gong,
 Harvey, Hebbia, Higgsfield, Perplexity, Ramp, Runway and Sierra among many others)
 and 325+ investors.

 Broad Themes – Token Optimization
 The most dominant themes were: 1. Consistent with our customer checks, these AI-
 native firms cited a renewed effort by users to better manage AI compute/token costs.
 As one prominent coding agent provider said, “every single customer conversation is
 about this”. The dominant lever was a shift to cheaper open source models, mostly
 Chinese but with several positive call-outs about Nvidia’s Nemotron model. While the
 AI-natives argued that the growth of the frontier models should also remain very strong,
 it was clear from these AI-native conversations that one key token optimization impact
 was a down-tiering to cheaper models. 2. The growth rates of the AI-natives, just in the
 last 6+ months, have been extraordinary and a) signal that enterprises ARE indeed
 leaning into AI (raising AI crowding-out concerns) and b) are a positive read to tech
 suppliers (Datadog, AI infra firms) serving this hyper-growth customer segment.

 Segment/Company-Specific Color
 We’d note: 1. SaaS/apps: While few, if any, of the AI-natives cited examples of
 customers using their tech to displace incumbent SaaS/apps products (good news),
 there were few, if any, call-outs of SaaS incumbents having material success selling their
 AI suites. 2. Coding/SDLC: One AI coding firm cited plans to “shift-right” from coding
 and automate more of the software development process, including building an
 alternative to Git repos and potentially issue tracking systems like Atlassian Jira (but NOT
 displace JFrog). 3. Security: The demand environment was characterized as steadily
 improving with more direct focus at the CEO/Board level (but without a pronounced 2Q
 inflection), growing focus on data security, and an evolving competitive landscape
 between incumbents and AI security specialists. 4. Datadog: One of Datadog’s rivals
 argued that it’s proving to be very tough to displace Datadog (they are focused on
 Splunk/Elastic instead). 5. Neo-Clouds: We were impressed with the number of AI-
 natives running on multiple “neo-cloud” AI infrastructure providers. 6. AI data centers:
 One of the prominent AI data center builders delivered an “on time-on budget”
 summary of their major data centers builds, good news for Oracle. 7. Edge AI
 inference: One AI-native said that “in 12 months, frontier-class models will be able to
